**Philosophy** ('love of wisdom' in [Ancient Greek](/wiki/Ancient_Greek
"Ancient Greek")) is a systematic study of general and fundamental questions
concerning topics like [existence](/wiki/Existence "Existence"),
[reason](/wiki/Reason "Reason"), [knowledge](/wiki/Knowledge "Knowledge"),
[value](/wiki/Value_\(ethics_and_social_sciences\) "Value \(ethics and social
sciences\)"), [mind](/wiki/Mind "Mind"), and [language](/wiki/Language
"Language"). It is a rational and critical inquiry that reflects on its
methods and assumptions.

Historically, many of the individual [sciences](/wiki/Science "Science"), such
as [physics](/wiki/Physics "Physics") and [psychology](/wiki/Psychology
"Psychology"), formed part of philosophy. However, they are considered
separate academic disciplines in the modern sense of the term. Influential
traditions in the [history of philosophy](/wiki/History_of_philosophy "History
of philosophy") include [Western](/wiki/Western_philosophy "Western
philosophy"), [Arabic–Persian](/wiki/Islamic_philosophy "Islamic philosophy"),
[Indian](/wiki/Indian_philosophy "Indian philosophy"), and [Chinese
philosophy](/wiki/Chinese_philosophy "Chinese philosophy"). Western philosophy
originated in [Ancient Greece](/wiki/Ancient_Greece "Ancient Greece") and
covers a wide area of philosophical subfields. A central topic in
Arabic–Persian philosophy is the relation between reason and
[revelation](/wiki/Revelation "Revelation"). Indian philosophy combines the
[spiritual](/wiki/Spirituality "Spirituality") problem of how to reach
[enlightenment](/wiki/Enlightenment_in_Buddhism "Enlightenment in Buddhism")
with the exploration of the nature of reality and the ways of arriving at
knowledge. Chinese philosophy focuses principally on practical issues about
right social conduct, government, and [self-cultivation](/wiki/Self-
cultivation "Self-cultivation").

Major branches of philosophy are [epistemology](/wiki/Epistemology
"Epistemology"), [ethics](/wiki/Ethics "Ethics"), [logic](/wiki/Logic
"Logic"), and [metaphysics](/wiki/Metaphysics "Metaphysics"). Epistemology
studies what knowledge is and how to acquire it. Ethics investigates moral
principles and what constitutes right conduct. Logic is the study of [correct
reasoning](/wiki/Logical_reasoning "Logical reasoning") and explores how good
[arguments](/wiki/Argument "Argument") can be distinguished from bad ones.
Metaphysics examines the most general features of [reality](/wiki/Reality
"Reality"), existence, [objects](/wiki/Object_\(philosophy\) "Object
\(philosophy\)"), and [properties](/wiki/Property_\(philosophy\) "Property
\(philosophy\)"). Other subfields are [aesthetics](/wiki/Aesthetics
"Aesthetics"), [philosophy of language](/wiki/Philosophy_of_language
"Philosophy of language"), [philosophy of mind](/wiki/Philosophy_of_mind
"Philosophy of mind"), [philosophy of religion](/wiki/Philosophy_of_religion
"Philosophy of religion"), [philosophy of science](/wiki/Philosophy_of_science
"Philosophy of science"), [philosophy of
mathematics](/wiki/Philosophy_of_mathematics "Philosophy of mathematics"),
[philosophy of history](/wiki/Philosophy_of_history "Philosophy of history"),
and [political philosophy](/wiki/Political_philosophy "Political philosophy").
Within each branch, there are competing [schools of
philosophy](/wiki/Schools_of_philosophy "Schools of philosophy") that promote
different principles, theories, or methods.

Philosophers use a great variety of methods to arrive at philosophical
knowledge. They include [conceptual analysis](/wiki/Conceptual_analysis
"Conceptual analysis"), reliance on [common sense](/wiki/Common_sense "Common
sense") and [intuitions](/wiki/Intuition "Intuition"), use of [thought
experiments](/wiki/Thought_experiments "Thought experiments"), analysis of
[ordinary language](/wiki/Ordinary_language "Ordinary language"), [description
of experience](/wiki/Phenomenology_\(philosophy\) "Phenomenology
\(philosophy\)"), and [critical questioning](/wiki/Socratic_questioning
"Socratic questioning"). Philosophy is related to many other fields, including
the sciences, [mathematics](/wiki/Mathematics "Mathematics"),
[business](/wiki/Business "Business"), [law](/wiki/Law "Law"), and
[journalism](/wiki/Journalism "Journalism"). It provides an
[interdisciplinary](/wiki/Interdisciplinary "Interdisciplinary") perspective
and studies the scope and fundamental concepts of these fields. It also
investigates their methods and ethical implications.

## Etymology

The word _philosophy_ comes from the [Ancient Greek](/wiki/Ancient_Greek
"Ancient Greek") words φίλος (_philos_) 'love' and σοφία
([_sophia_](/wiki/Sophia_\(wisdom\)#Greek_and_Hellenistic_tradition "Sophia
\(wisdom\)")) 'wisdom'.[2][a] Some sources say that the term was coined by the
[pre-Socratic](/wiki/Pre-Socratic "Pre-Socratic") philosopher
[Pythagoras](/wiki/Pythagoras "Pythagoras"), but this is not certain.[4]

[![Wood engraving of Isaac Newton under an apple
tree](//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Doctor_Zirkel_follows_Newton%27s_famous_steps_under_the_fabled_Wellcome_V0011942.jpg/250px-
Doctor_Zirkel_follows_Newton%27s_famous_steps_under_the_fabled_Wellcome_V0011942.jpg)](/wiki/File:Doctor_Zirkel_follows_Newton%27s_famous_steps_under_the_fabled_Wellcome_V0011942.jpg)Physics
was originally part of philosophy, like [Isaac Newton](/wiki/Isaac_Newton
"Isaac Newton")'s observation of how [gravity affects falling
apples](/wiki/Isaac_Newton%27s_apple_tree "Isaac Newton's apple tree").

The word entered the English language primarily from [Old
French](/wiki/Old_French "Old French") and [Anglo-Norman](/wiki/Anglo-
Norman_language "Anglo-Norman language") starting around 1175 CE. The French
_philosophie_ is itself a borrowing from the Latin _philosophia_. The term
_philosophy_ acquired the meanings of "advanced study of the speculative
subjects ([logic](/wiki/Logic "Logic"), [ethics](/wiki/Ethics "Ethics"),
[physics](/wiki/Physics "Physics"), and [metaphysics](/wiki/Metaphysics
"Metaphysics"))", "deep wisdom consisting of love of truth and virtuous
living", "profound learning as transmitted by the ancient writers", and "the
study of the fundamental nature of [knowledge](/wiki/Knowledge "Knowledge"),
[reality](/wiki/Reality "Reality"), and [existence](/wiki/Existence
"Existence"), and the basic limits of human understanding".[5]

Before the modern age, the term _philosophy_ was used in a wide sense. It
included most forms of [rational](/wiki/Rational "Rational") inquiry, such as
the individual [sciences](/wiki/Sciences "Sciences"), as its
subdisciplines.[6] For instance, [natural philosophy](/wiki/Natural_philosophy
"Natural philosophy") was a major branch of philosophy.[7] This branch of
philosophy encompassed a wide range of fields, including disciplines like
physics, [chemistry](/wiki/Chemistry "Chemistry"), and [biology](/wiki/Biology
"Biology").[8] An example of this usage is the 1687 book _[Philosophiæ
Naturalis Principia
Mathematica](/wiki/Philosophi%C3%A6_Naturalis_Principia_Mathematica
"Philosophiæ Naturalis Principia Mathematica")_ by [Isaac
Newton](/wiki/Isaac_Newton "Isaac Newton"). This book referred to natural
philosophy in its title, but it is today considered a book of physics.[9]

The meaning of _philosophy_ changed toward the end of the modern period when
it acquired the more narrow meaning common today. In this new sense, the term
is mainly associated with disciplines like metaphysics, epistemology, and
ethics. Among other topics, it covers the rational study of reality,
knowledge, and values. It is distinguished from other disciplines of rational
inquiry such as the empirical sciences and [mathematics](/wiki/Mathematics
"Mathematics").[10]

## Conceptions of philosophy

See also: [Metaphilosophy](/wiki/Metaphilosophy "Metaphilosophy")

### General conception

The practice of philosophy is characterized by several general features: it is
a form of rational inquiry, it aims to be systematic, and it tends to
critically reflect on its own methods and presuppositions.[11] It requires
attentively thinking long and carefully about the provocative, vexing, and
enduring problems central to the human condition.[12]

The philosophical pursuit of wisdom involves asking general and fundamental
questions. It often does not result in straightforward answers but may help a
person to better understand the topic, examine their life, dispel confusion,
and overcome prejudices and self-deceptive ideas associated with common
sense.[13] For example, [Socrates](/wiki/Socrates "Socrates") stated that
"[the unexamined life is not worth
living](/wiki/The_unexamined_life_is_not_worth_living "The unexamined life is
not worth living")" to highlight the role of philosophical inquiry in
understanding one's own existence.[14][15] And according to [Bertrand
Russell](/wiki/Bertrand_Russell "Bertrand Russell"), "the man who has no
tincture of philosophy goes through life imprisoned in the prejudices derived
from common sense, from the habitual beliefs of his age or his nation, and
from convictions which have grown up in his mind without the cooperation or
consent of his deliberate reason."[16]

### Academic definitions

Main article: [Definitions of philosophy](/wiki/Definitions_of_philosophy
"Definitions of philosophy")

Attempts to provide more precise definitions of philosophy are
controversial[17] and are studied in [metaphilosophy](/wiki/Metaphilosophy
"Metaphilosophy").[18] Some approaches argue that there is a set of essential
features shared by all parts of philosophy. Others see only weaker family
resemblances or contend that it is merely an empty blanket term.[19] Precise
definitions are often only accepted by theorists belonging to a certain
[philosophical movement](/wiki/Philosophical_movement "Philosophical
movement") and are revisionistic according to Søren Overgaard et al. in that
many presumed parts of philosophy would not deserve the title "philosophy" if
they were true.[20]

Some definitions characterize philosophy in relation to its method, like pure
reasoning. Others focus on its topic, for example, as the study of the biggest
patterns of the world as a whole or as the attempt to answer the big
questions.[21] Such an approach is pursued by [Immanuel
Kant](/wiki/Immanuel_Kant "Immanuel Kant"), who holds that the task of
philosophy is united by four questions: "What can I know?"; "What should I
do?"; "What may I hope?"; and "What is the human being?"[22] Both approaches
have the problem that they are usually either too wide, by including non-
philosophical disciplines, or too narrow, by excluding some philosophical sub-
disciplines.[23]

Many definitions of philosophy emphasize its intimate relation to science.[24]
In this sense, philosophy is sometimes understood as a proper science in its
own right. According to some [naturalistic
philosophers](/wiki/Naturalism_\(philosophy\) "Naturalism \(philosophy\)"),
such as [W. V. O. Quine](/wiki/W._V._O._Quine "W. V. O. Quine"), philosophy is
an empirical yet abstract science that is concerned with wide-ranging
empirical patterns instead of particular observations.[25] Science-based
definitions usually face the problem of explaining why philosophy in its long
history has not progressed to the same extent or in the same way as the
sciences.[26] This problem is avoided by seeing philosophy as an immature or
provisional science whose subdisciplines cease to be philosophy once they have
fully developed.[27] In this sense, philosophy is sometimes described as "the
midwife of the sciences".[28]

Other definitions focus on the contrast between science and philosophy. A
common theme among many such conceptions is that philosophy is concerned with
[meaning](/wiki/Meaning_\(philosophy\) "Meaning \(philosophy\)"),
[understanding](/wiki/Understanding "Understanding"), or the clarification of
language.[29] According to one view, philosophy is [conceptual
analysis](/wiki/Conceptual_analysis "Conceptual analysis"), which involves
finding the [necessary and sufficient
conditions](/wiki/Necessary_and_sufficient_conditions "Necessary and
sufficient conditions") for the application of concepts.[30] Another
definition characterizes philosophy as _[thinking](/wiki/Thinking "Thinking")
about thinking_ to emphasize its self-critical, reflective nature.[31] A
further approach presents philosophy as a [linguistic](/wiki/Linguistic
"Linguistic") therapy. According to [Ludwig
Wittgenstein](/wiki/Ludwig_Wittgenstein "Ludwig Wittgenstein"), for instance,
philosophy aims at dispelling misunderstandings to which humans are
susceptible due to the confusing structure of [ordinary
language](/wiki/Natural_language "Natural language").[32]

[Phenomenologists](/wiki/Phenomenology_\(philosophy\) "Phenomenology
\(philosophy\)"), such as [Edmund Husserl](/wiki/Edmund_Husserl "Edmund
Husserl"), characterize philosophy as a "rigorous science" investigating
[essences](/wiki/Essence "Essence").[33] They practice a radical
[suspension](/wiki/Epoche "Epoche") of theoretical assumptions about reality
to get back to the "things themselves", that is, as originally given in
experience. They contend that this base-level of experience provides the
foundation for higher-order theoretical knowledge, and that one needs to
understand the former to understand the latter.[34]

An early approach found in [ancient Greek](/wiki/Ancient_Greek_philosophy
"Ancient Greek philosophy") and [Roman philosophy](/wiki/Roman_philosophy
"Roman philosophy") is that philosophy is the spiritual practice of developing
one's rational capacities.[35] This practice is an expression of the
philosopher's love of wisdom and has the aim of improving one's [well-
being](/wiki/Well-being "Well-being") by leading a reflective life.[36] For
example, the [Stoics](/wiki/Stoics "Stoics") saw philosophy as an exercise to
train the mind and thereby achieve [eudaimonia](/wiki/Eudaimonia "Eudaimonia")
and flourish in life.[37]

## History

Main article: [History of philosophy](/wiki/History_of_philosophy "History of
philosophy")

As a discipline, the history of philosophy aims to provide a systematic and
chronological exposition of philosophical concepts and doctrines.[38] Some
theorists see it as a part of [intellectual
history](/wiki/Intellectual_history "Intellectual history"), but it also
investigates questions not covered by intellectual history such as whether the
theories of past philosophers are true and have remained philosophically
relevant.[39] The history of philosophy is primarily concerned with theories
based on rational inquiry and argumentation; some historians understand it in
a looser sense that includes [myths](/wiki/Myth "Myth"), [religious
teachings](/wiki/Religion "Religion"), and proverbial lore.[40]

Influential traditions in the history of philosophy include
[Western](/wiki/Western_philosophy "Western philosophy"),
[Arabic–Persian](/wiki/Islamic_philosophy "Islamic philosophy"),
[Indian](/wiki/Indian_philosophy "Indian philosophy"), and [Chinese
philosophy](/wiki/Chinese_philosophy "Chinese philosophy"). Other
philosophical traditions are [Japanese philosophy](/wiki/Japanese_philosophy
"Japanese philosophy"), [Latin American
philosophy](/wiki/Latin_American_philosophy "Latin American philosophy"), and
[African philosophy](/wiki/African_philosophy "African philosophy").[41]

### Western

Main article: [Western philosophy](/wiki/Western_philosophy "Western
philosophy")

[![Bust of
Aristotle](//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/Aristotle_Altemps_Inv8575.jpg/250px-
Aristotle_Altemps_Inv8575.jpg)](/wiki/File:Aristotle_Altemps_Inv8575.jpg)[Aristotle](/wiki/Aristotle
"Aristotle") was a major figure in ancient philosophy and developed a
comprehensive system of thought including metaphysics, logic, ethics,
politics, and natural science.[42]

Western philosophy originated in [Ancient Greece](/wiki/Ancient_Greece
"Ancient Greece") in the 6th century BCE with the [pre-Socratics](/wiki/Pre-
Socratics "Pre-Socratics"). They attempted to provide rational explanations of
the [cosmos](/wiki/Cosmos "Cosmos") as a whole.[43] The philosophy following
them was shaped by [Socrates](/wiki/Socrates "Socrates") (469–399 BCE),
[Plato](/wiki/Plato "Plato") (427–347 BCE), and [Aristotle](/wiki/Aristotle
"Aristotle") (384–322 BCE). They expanded the range of topics to questions
like [how people should act](/wiki/Ethics "Ethics"), [how to arrive at
knowledge](/wiki/Epistemology "Epistemology"), and what the [nature of
reality](/wiki/Metaphysics "Metaphysics") and [mind](/wiki/Mind "Mind")
is.[44] The later part of the ancient period was marked by the emergence of
philosophical movements, for example, [Epicureanism](/wiki/Epicureanism
"Epicureanism"), [Stoicism](/wiki/Stoicism "Stoicism"),
[Skepticism](/wiki/Skepticism "Skepticism"), and
[Neoplatonism](/wiki/Neoplatonism "Neoplatonism").[45] The medieval period
started in the 5th century CE. Its focus was on religious topics and many
thinkers used ancient philosophy to explain and further elaborate [Christian
doctrines](/wiki/Christian_doctrine "Christian doctrine").[46][47]

The [Renaissance](/wiki/Renaissance "Renaissance") period started in the 14th
century and saw a renewed interest in schools of ancient philosophy, in
particular [Platonism](/wiki/Platonism "Platonism").
[Humanism](/wiki/Renaissance_humanism "Renaissance humanism") also emerged in
this period.[48] The modern period started in the 17th century. One of its
central concerns was how philosophical and scientific knowledge are created.
Specific importance was given to the [role of reason](/wiki/Rationalism
"Rationalism") and [sensory experience](/wiki/Empiricism "Empiricism").[49]
Many of these innovations were used in the [Enlightenment
movement](/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment "Age of Enlightenment") to challenge
traditional authorities.[50] Several attempts to develop comprehensive systems
of philosophy were made in the 19th century, for instance, by [German
idealism](/wiki/German_idealism "German idealism") and [Marxism](/wiki/Marxism
"Marxism").[51] Influential developments in 20th-century philosophy were the
emergence and application of [formal logic](/wiki/Formal_logic "Formal
logic"), the focus on the [role of language](/wiki/Linguistic_turn "Linguistic
turn") as well as [pragmatism](/wiki/Pragmatism "Pragmatism"), and movements
in [continental philosophy](/wiki/Continental_philosophy "Continental
philosophy") like phenomenology, [existentialism](/wiki/Existentialism
"Existentialism"), and [post-structuralism](/wiki/Post-structuralism "Post-
structuralism").[52] The 20th century saw a rapid expansion of academic
philosophy in terms of the number of philosophical publications and
philosophers working at [academic institutions](/wiki/Academic_institution
"Academic institution").[53] There was also a noticeable growth in the number
of [female philosophers](/wiki/Female_philosophers "Female philosophers"), but
they still remained underrepresented.[54]

### Arabic–Persian

Main articles: [Islamic philosophy](/wiki/Islamic_philosophy "Islamic
philosophy") and [Iranian philosophy](/wiki/Iranian_philosophy "Iranian
philosophy")

[![Portrait of Avicenna on a Silver
Vase](//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/Avicenna_Portrait_on_Silver_Vase_-
_Museum_at_BuAli_Sina_%28Avicenna%29_Mausoleum_-_Hamadan_-
_Western_Iran_%287423560860%29.jpg/250px-Avicenna_Portrait_on_Silver_Vase_-
_Museum_at_BuAli_Sina_%28Avicenna%29_Mausoleum_-_Hamadan_-
_Western_Iran_%287423560860%29.jpg)](/wiki/File:Avicenna_Portrait_on_Silver_Vase_-
_Museum_at_BuAli_Sina_\(Avicenna\)_Mausoleum_-_Hamadan_-
_Western_Iran_\(7423560860\).jpg)Portrait of [Avicenna](/wiki/Avicenna
"Avicenna"), one of the most influential philosophers of the [Islamic Golden
Age](/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age "Islamic Golden Age")

Arabic–Persian philosophy arose in the early 9th century CE as a response to
discussions in the [Islamic theological tradition](/wiki/Kalam "Kalam"). Its
classical period lasted until the 12th century CE and was strongly influenced
by ancient Greek philosophers. It employed their ideas to elaborate and
interpret the teachings of the [Quran](/wiki/Quran "Quran").[55]

[Al-Kindi](/wiki/Al-Kindi "Al-Kindi") (801–873 CE) is usually regarded as the
first philosopher of this tradition. He translated and interpreted many works
of Aristotle and Neoplatonists in his attempt to show that there is a harmony
between [reason](/wiki/Reason "Reason") and [faith](/wiki/Faith "Faith").[56]
[Avicenna](/wiki/Avicenna "Avicenna") (980–1037 CE) also followed this goal
and developed a comprehensive philosophical system to provide a rational
understanding of reality encompassing science, religion, and mysticism.[57]
[Al-Ghazali](/wiki/Al-Ghazali "Al-Ghazali") (1058–1111 CE) was a strong critic
of the idea that reason can arrive at a true understanding of reality and God.
He formulated a detailed [critique of
philosophy](/wiki/The_Incoherence_of_the_Philosophers "The Incoherence of the
Philosophers") and tried to assign philosophy a more limited place besides the
teachings of the Quran and mystical insight.[58] Following Al-Ghazali and the
end of the classical period, the influence of philosophical inquiry waned.[59]
[Mulla Sadra](/wiki/Mulla_Sadra "Mulla Sadra") (1571–1636 CE) is often
regarded as one of the most influential philosophers of the subsequent
period.[60] The increasing influence of Western thought and institutions in
the 19th and 20th centuries gave rise to the intellectual movement of [Islamic
modernism](/wiki/Islamic_modernism "Islamic modernism"), which aims to
understand the relation between traditional Islamic beliefs and modernity.[61]

### Indian

Main article: [Indian philosophy](/wiki/Indian_philosophy "Indian philosophy")

[![Painting of Adi
Shankara](//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Raja_Ravi_Varma_-
_Sankaracharya_-_cropped.png/250px-Raja_Ravi_Varma_-_Sankaracharya_-
_cropped.png)](/wiki/File:Raja_Ravi_Varma_-_Sankaracharya_-_cropped.png)[Adi
Shankara](/wiki/Adi_Shankara "Adi Shankara") developed the
[monistic](/wiki/Monism "Monism") view of [Advaita
Vedanta](/wiki/Advaita_Vedanta "Advaita Vedanta"), stating that the existence
of a plurality of distinct entities is an [illusion](/wiki/Maya_\(religion\)
"Maya \(religion\)").

One of the distinguishing features of Indian philosophy is that it integrates
the exploration of the nature of reality, the ways of arriving at knowledge,
and the [spiritual](/wiki/Spirituality "Spirituality") question of how to
reach [enlightenment](/wiki/Enlightenment_in_Buddhism "Enlightenment in
Buddhism").[62] It started around 900 BCE when the [Vedas](/wiki/Vedas
"Vedas") were written. They are the foundational scriptures of
[Hinduism](/wiki/Hinduism "Hinduism") and contemplate issues concerning the
relation between the [self](/wiki/%C4%80tman_\(Hinduism\) "Ātman
\(Hinduism\)") and [ultimate reality](/wiki/Brahman "Brahman") as well as the
question of how [souls](/wiki/Jiva "Jiva") are reborn based on their [past
actions](/wiki/Karma "Karma").[63] This period also saw the emergence of non-
Vedic teachings, like [Buddhism](/wiki/Buddhism "Buddhism") and
[Jainism](/wiki/Jainism "Jainism").[64] Buddhism was founded by [Gautama
Siddhartha](/wiki/Gautama_Siddhartha "Gautama Siddhartha") (563–483 BCE), who
challenged the Vedic idea of a [permanent self](/wiki/Anicca "Anicca") and
proposed [a path](/wiki/Noble_Eightfold_Path "Noble Eightfold Path") to
liberate oneself from [suffering](/wiki/Du%E1%B8%A5kha "Duḥkha").[64] Jainism
was founded by [Mahavira](/wiki/Mahavira "Mahavira") (599–527 BCE), who
emphasized [non-violence](/wiki/Ahi%E1%B9%83s%C4%81 "Ahiṃsā") as well as
respect toward all forms of life.[65]

The subsequent classical period started roughly 200 BCE[b] and was
characterized by the emergence of the six [orthodox schools of
Hinduism](/wiki/Astika "Astika"): [Nyāyá](/wiki/Nyaya "Nyaya"),
[Vaiśeṣika](/wiki/Vai%C5%9Be%E1%B9%A3ika "Vaiśeṣika"),
[Sāṃkhya](/wiki/S%C4%81%E1%B9%83khya "Sāṃkhya"), [Yoga](/wiki/Yoga "Yoga"),
[Mīmāṃsā](/wiki/M%C4%ABm%C4%81%E1%B9%83s%C4%81 "Mīmāṃsā"), and
[Vedanta](/wiki/Vedanta "Vedanta").[67] The school of [Advaita
Vedanta](/wiki/Advaita_Vedanta "Advaita Vedanta") developed later in this
period. It was systematized by [Adi Shankara](/wiki/Adi_Shankara "Adi
Shankara") (c. 700–750 CE), who held that [everything is one](/wiki/Monism
"Monism") and that the impression of a universe consisting of many distinct
entities is an [illusion](/wiki/Maya_\(religion\) "Maya \(religion\)").[68] A
slightly different perspective was defended by [Ramanuja](/wiki/Ramanuja
"Ramanuja") (1017–1137 CE),[c] who founded the school of [Vishishtadvaita
Vedanta](/wiki/Vishishtadvaita_Vedanta "Vishishtadvaita Vedanta") and argued
that individual entities are real as aspects or parts of the underlying
unity.[70] He also helped to popularize the [Bhakti
movement](/wiki/Bhakti_movement "Bhakti movement"), which taught [devotion
toward the divine](/wiki/Bhakti "Bhakti") as a spiritual path and lasted until
the 17th to 18th centuries CE.[71] The modern period began roughly 1800 CE and
was shaped by encounters with Western thought.[72] Philosophers tried to
formulate comprehensive systems to harmonize diverse philosophical and
religious teachings. For example, [Swami Vivekananda](/wiki/Swami_Vivekananda
"Swami Vivekananda") (1863–1902 CE) used the teachings of Advaita Vedanta to
argue that all the different religions are valid paths toward the one
divine.[73]

### Chinese

Main article: [Chinese philosophy](/wiki/Chinese_philosophy "Chinese
philosophy")

[![Painting of
Confucius](//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Half_Portraits_of_the_Great_Sage_and_Virtuous_Men_of_Old_-
_Confucius.jpg/250px-
Half_Portraits_of_the_Great_Sage_and_Virtuous_Men_of_Old_-
_Confucius.jpg)](/wiki/File:Half_Portraits_of_the_Great_Sage_and_Virtuous_Men_of_Old_-
_Confucius.jpg)The teachings of [Confucius](/wiki/Confucius "Confucius") on
ethics and society shaped subsequent Chinese philosophy.

Chinese philosophy is particularly interested in practical questions
associated with right social conduct, government, and [self-
cultivation](/wiki/Self-cultivation "Self-cultivation").[74] Many [schools of
thought](/wiki/Hundred_Schools_of_Thought "Hundred Schools of Thought")
emerged in the 6th century BCE in competing attempts to resolve the political
turbulence of that period. The most prominent among them were
[Confucianism](/wiki/Confucianism "Confucianism") and [Daoism](/wiki/Daoism
"Daoism").[75] Confucianism was founded by [Confucius](/wiki/Confucius
"Confucius") (551–479 BCE). It focused on different forms of moral
[virtues](/wiki/Virtue "Virtue") and explored how they lead to harmony in
society.[76] Daoism was founded by [Laozi](/wiki/Laozi "Laozi") (6th century
BCE) and examined how humans can live in harmony with nature by following the
[Dao](/wiki/Dao "Dao") or the natural order of the universe.[77] Other
influential early schools of thought were [Mohism](/wiki/Mohism "Mohism"),
which developed an early form of altruistic
[consequentialism](/wiki/Consequentialism "Consequentialism"),[78] and
[Legalism](/wiki/Legalism_\(Chinese_philosophy\) "Legalism \(Chinese
philosophy\)"), which emphasized the importance of a strong state and strict
laws.[79]

Buddhism was introduced to China in the 1st century CE and diversified into
[new forms of Buddhism](/wiki/Chinese_Buddhism "Chinese Buddhism").[80]
Starting in the 3rd century CE, the school of [Xuanxue](/wiki/Xuanxue
"Xuanxue") emerged. It interpreted earlier Daoist works with a specific
emphasis on metaphysical explanations.[80] [Neo-Confucianism](/wiki/Neo-
Confucianism "Neo-Confucianism") developed in the 11th century CE. It
systematized previous Confucian teachings and sought a metaphysical foundation
of ethics.[81] The modern period in Chinese philosophy began in the early 20th
century and was shaped by the influence of and reactions to Western
philosophy. The emergence of [Chinese Marxism](/wiki/Chinese_Marxism "Chinese
Marxism")—which focused on [class struggle](/wiki/Class_struggle "Class
struggle"), [socialism](/wiki/Socialism "Socialism"), and
[communism](/wiki/Communism "Communism")—resulted in a significant
transformation of the political landscape.[82] Another development was the
emergence of [New Confucianism](/wiki/New_Confucianism "New Confucianism"),
which aims to modernize and rethink Confucian teachings to explore their
compatibility with democratic ideals and modern science.[83]

### Other traditions

Traditional Japanese philosophy assimilated and synthesized ideas from
different traditions, including the indigenous [Shinto](/wiki/Shinto "Shinto")
religion and Chinese and Indian thought in the forms of Confucianism and
Buddhism, both of which entered Japan in the 6th and 7th centuries. Its
practice is characterized by active interaction with reality rather than
disengaged examination.[84] Neo-Confucianism became an influential school of
thought in the 16th century and the following [Edo period](/wiki/Edo_period
"Edo period") and prompted a greater focus on language and the natural
world.[85] The [Kyoto School](/wiki/Kyoto_School "Kyoto School") emerged in
the 20th century and integrated Eastern spirituality with Western philosophy
in its exploration of concepts like absolute nothingness (_zettai-mu_), place
(_basho_), and the [self](/wiki/Self "Self").[86]

Latin American philosophy in the [pre-colonial period](/wiki/Pre-Columbian_era
"Pre-Columbian era") was practiced by indigenous civilizations and explored
questions concerning the nature of reality and the role of humans.[87] It has
similarities to [indigenous North American
philosophy](/wiki/Indigenous_American_philosophy "Indigenous American
philosophy"), which covered themes such as the interconnectedness of all
things.[88] Latin American philosophy during the [colonial
period](/wiki/European_colonization_of_the_Americas "European colonization of
the Americas"), starting around 1550, was dominated by religious philosophy in
the form of [scholasticism](/wiki/Scholasticism "Scholasticism"). Influential
topics in the post-colonial period were [positivism](/wiki/Positivism
"Positivism"), the [philosophy of liberation](/wiki/Philosophy_of_liberation
"Philosophy of liberation"), and the exploration of identity and culture.[89]

Early African philosophy was primarily conducted and transmitted orally. It
focused on community, morality, and ancestral ideas, encompassing folklore,
wise sayings, religious ideas, and philosophical concepts like
[Ubuntu](/wiki/Ubuntu_philosophy "Ubuntu philosophy").[90] Systematic African
philosophy emerged at the beginning of the 20th century. It discusses topics
such as [ethnophilosophy](/wiki/Ethnophilosophy "Ethnophilosophy"),
[négritude](/wiki/N%C3%A9gritude "Négritude"), [pan-Africanism](/wiki/Pan-
Africanism "Pan-Africanism"), Marxism, [postcolonialism](/wiki/Postcolonialism
"Postcolonialism"), the role of cultural identity,
[relativism](/wiki/Relativism "Relativism"), [African
epistemology](/wiki/African_philosophy#African_epistemology "African
philosophy"), and the critique of [Eurocentrism](/wiki/Eurocentrism
"Eurocentrism").[91]

## Core branches

See also: [Outline of philosophy § Branches of
philosophy](/wiki/Outline_of_philosophy#Branches_of_philosophy "Outline of
philosophy"), and [Outline of philosophy § Philosophical schools of
thought](/wiki/Outline_of_philosophy#Philosophical_schools_of_thought "Outline
of philosophy")

Philosophical questions can be grouped into several branches. These groupings
allow philosophers to focus on a set of similar topics and interact with other
thinkers who are interested in the same questions. Epistemology, ethics,
logic, and metaphysics are sometimes listed as the main branches.[92] There
are many other subfields besides them and the different divisions are neither
exhaustive nor mutually exclusive. For example, political philosophy, ethics,
and [aesthetics](/wiki/Aesthetics "Aesthetics") are sometimes linked under the
general heading of [value theory](/wiki/Value_theory "Value theory") as they
investigate [normative](/wiki/Normative "Normative") or evaluative
aspects.[93] Furthermore, philosophical inquiry sometimes overlaps with other
disciplines in the natural and social sciences, religion, and mathematics.[94]

### Epistemology

Main article: [Epistemology](/wiki/Epistemology "Epistemology")

Epistemology is the branch of philosophy that studies knowledge. It is also
known as _theory of knowledge_ and aims to understand what knowledge is, how
it arises, what its limits are, and what value it has. It further examines the
nature of [truth](/wiki/Truth "Truth"), [belief](/wiki/Belief "Belief"),
[justification](/wiki/Justification_\(epistemology\) "Justification
\(epistemology\)"), and [rationality](/wiki/Reason "Reason").[95] Some of the
questions addressed by epistemologists include "By what method(s) can one
acquire knowledge?"; "How is truth established?"; and "Can we prove causal
relations?"[96]

Epistemology is primarily interested in [declarative
knowledge](/wiki/Declarative_knowledge "Declarative knowledge") or knowledge
of facts, like knowing that Princess Diana died in 1997. But it also
investigates [practical knowledge](/wiki/Practical_knowledge "Practical
knowledge"), such as knowing how to ride a bicycle, and [knowledge by
acquaintance](/wiki/Knowledge_by_acquaintance "Knowledge by acquaintance"),
for example, knowing a celebrity personally.[97]

One area in epistemology is the _[analysis of
knowledge](/wiki/Analysis_of_knowledge "Analysis of knowledge")_. It assumes
that declarative knowledge is a combination of different parts and attempts to
identify what those parts are. An influential theory in this area claims that
knowledge has three components: it is a _belief_ that is _justified_ and
_true_. This theory is controversial and the difficulties associated with it
are known as the [Gettier problem](/wiki/Gettier_problem "Gettier
problem").[98] Alternative views state that knowledge requires additional
components, like the absence of luck; different components, like the
manifestation of [cognitive virtues](/wiki/Epistemic_virtue "Epistemic
virtue") instead of justification; or they deny that knowledge can be analyzed
in terms of other phenomena.[99]

Another area in epistemology asks how people acquire knowledge. Often-
discussed sources of knowledge are [perception](/wiki/Perception
"Perception"), [introspection](/wiki/Introspection "Introspection"),
[memory](/wiki/Memory "Memory"), [inference](/wiki/Inference "Inference"), and
[testimony](/wiki/Testimony "Testimony").[100] According to
[empiricists](/wiki/Empiricists "Empiricists"), all knowledge is based on some
form of experience. Rationalists reject this view and hold that some forms of
knowledge, like [innate knowledge](/wiki/Innate_knowledge "Innate knowledge"),
are not acquired through experience.[101] The [regress
problem](/wiki/Regress_problem "Regress problem") is a common issue in
relation to the sources of knowledge and the justification they offer. It is
based on the idea that beliefs require some kind of reason or evidence to be
justified. The problem is that the source of justification may itself be in
need of another source of justification. This leads to an [infinite
regress](/wiki/Infinite_regress "Infinite regress") or [circular
reasoning](/wiki/Circular_reasoning "Circular reasoning").
[Foundationalists](/wiki/Foundationalist "Foundationalist") avoid this
conclusion by arguing that some sources can provide justification without
requiring justification themselves.[102] Another solution is presented by
[coherentists](/wiki/Coherentist "Coherentist"), who state that a belief is
justified if it coheres with other beliefs of the person.[103]

Many discussions in epistemology touch on the topic of [philosophical
skepticism](/wiki/Philosophical_skepticism "Philosophical skepticism"), which
raises doubts about some or all claims to knowledge. These doubts are often
based on the idea that knowledge requires absolute certainty and that humans
are unable to acquire it.[104]

### Ethics

Main article: [Ethics](/wiki/Ethics "Ethics")

[![Drawing of John Stuart
Mill](//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/JohnStuartMill.jpg/250px-
JohnStuartMill.jpg)](/wiki/File:JohnStuartMill.jpg)"The utilitarian doctrine
is, that happiness is desirable, and the only thing desirable, as an end; all
other things being only desirable as means to that end." — [John Stuart
Mill](/wiki/John_Stuart_Mill "John Stuart Mill"),
_[Utilitarianism](/wiki/Utilitarianism_\(book\) "Utilitarianism \(book\)")_
(1863)[105]

Ethics, also known as moral philosophy, studies what constitutes right
[conduct](/wiki/Action_\(philosophy\) "Action \(philosophy\)"). It is also
concerned with the moral [evaluation](/wiki/Values_\(philosophy\) "Values
\(philosophy\)") of character traits and institutions. It explores what the
standards of [morality](/wiki/Morality "Morality") are and how to live a good
life.[106] Philosophical ethics addresses such basic questions as "Are moral
obligations relative?"; "Which has priority: well-being or obligation?"; and
"What gives life meaning?"[107]

The main branches of ethics are [meta-ethics](/wiki/Meta-ethics "Meta-
ethics"), [normative ethics](/wiki/Normative_ethics "Normative ethics"), and
[applied ethics](/wiki/Applied_ethics "Applied ethics").[108] Meta-ethics asks
abstract questions about the nature and sources of morality. It analyzes the
meaning of ethical concepts, like _right action_ and
_[obligation](/wiki/Obligation "Obligation")_. It also investigates whether
ethical theories can be [true in an absolute sense](/wiki/Moral_relativism
"Moral relativism") and how to acquire knowledge of them.[109] Normative
ethics encompasses general theories of how to distinguish between right and
wrong conduct. It helps guide moral decisions by examining what moral
obligations and rights people have. Applied ethics studies the consequences of
the general theories developed by normative ethics in specific situations, for
example, in the workplace or for medical treatments.[110]

Within contemporary normative ethics, consequentialism,
[deontology](/wiki/Deontology "Deontology"), and [virtue
ethics](/wiki/Virtue_ethics "Virtue ethics") are influential schools of
thought.[111] _Consequentialists_ judge actions based on their consequences.
One such view is [utilitarianism](/wiki/Utilitarianism "Utilitarianism"),
which argues that actions should increase overall happiness while minimizing
suffering. _Deontologists_ judge actions based on whether they follow moral
duties, such as abstaining from lying or killing. According to them, what
matters is that actions are in tune with those duties and not what
consequences they have. _Virtue theorists_ judge actions based on how the
moral character of the agent is expressed. According to this view, actions
should conform to what an ideally virtuous agent would do by manifesting
virtues like [generosity](/wiki/Generosity "Generosity") and
[honesty](/wiki/Honesty "Honesty").[112]

### Logic

Main article: [Logic](/wiki/Logic "Logic")

Logic is the study of [correct reasoning](/wiki/Logical_reasoning "Logical
reasoning"). It aims to understand how to distinguish good from bad
[arguments](/wiki/Argument "Argument").[113] It is usually divided into formal
and [informal logic](/wiki/Informal_logic "Informal logic"). Formal logic uses
[artificial languages](/wiki/Formal_language "Formal language") with a precise
symbolic representation to investigate arguments. In its search for exact
criteria, it examines the structure of arguments to determine whether they are
correct or incorrect. Informal logic uses non-formal criteria and standards to
assess the correctness of arguments. It relies on additional factors such as
content and context.[114]

Logic examines a variety of arguments. [Deductive
arguments](/wiki/Deductive_arguments "Deductive arguments") are mainly studied
by formal logic. An argument is deductively [valid](/wiki/Validity_\(logic\)
"Validity \(logic\)") if the truth of its [premises](/wiki/Premise "Premise")
ensures the truth of its conclusion. Deductively valid arguments follow a
[rule of inference](/wiki/Rule_of_inference "Rule of inference"), like _[modus
ponens](/wiki/Modus_ponens "Modus ponens")_ , which has the following [logical
form](/wiki/Logical_form "Logical form"): "_p_ ; if _p_ then _q_ ; therefore
_q_ ". An example is the argument "today is Sunday; if today is Sunday then I
don't have to go to work today; therefore I don't have to go to work
today".[115]

The premises of non-deductive arguments also support their conclusion,
although this support does not guarantee that the conclusion is true.[116] One
form is [inductive reasoning](/wiki/Inductive_reasoning "Inductive
reasoning"). It starts from a set of individual cases and uses generalization
to arrive at a universal law governing all cases. An example is the inference
that "all ravens are black" based on observations of many individual black
ravens.[117] Another form is [abductive reasoning](/wiki/Abductive_reasoning
"Abductive reasoning"). It starts from an observation and concludes that the
best explanation of this observation must be true. This happens, for example,
when a doctor diagnoses a disease based on the observed symptoms.[118]

Logic also investigates incorrect forms of reasoning. They are called
_[fallacies](/wiki/Fallacies "Fallacies")_ and are divided into
[formal](/wiki/Formal_fallacy "Formal fallacy") and [informal
fallacies](/wiki/Informal_fallacies "Informal fallacies") based on whether the
source of the error lies only in the form of the argument or also in its
content and context.[119]

### Metaphysics

Main article: [Metaphysics](/wiki/Metaphysics "Metaphysics")

[![Incunabulum showing the beginning of Aristotle's
Metaphysics](//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bb/Aristotle%2C_Metaphysics%2C_Incunabulum.jpg/250px-
Aristotle%2C_Metaphysics%2C_Incunabulum.jpg)](/wiki/File:Aristotle,_Metaphysics,_Incunabulum.jpg)The
beginning of [Aristotle's _Metaphysics_](/wiki/Metaphysics_\(Aristotle\)
"Metaphysics \(Aristotle\)") in an [incunabulum](/wiki/Incunabulum
"Incunabulum") decorated with hand-painted miniatures

Metaphysics is the study of the most general features of
[reality](/wiki/Reality "Reality"), such as existence,
[objects](/wiki/Object_\(philosophy\) "Object \(philosophy\)") and their
[properties](/wiki/Property_\(philosophy\) "Property \(philosophy\)"), [wholes
and their parts](/wiki/Mereology "Mereology"), [space](/wiki/Space "Space")
and [time](/wiki/Time "Time"), [events](/wiki/Event_\(philosophy\) "Event
\(philosophy\)"), and [causation](/wiki/Causality "Causality").[120] There are
disagreements about the precise definition of the term and its meaning has
changed throughout the ages.[121] Metaphysicians attempt to answer basic
questions including "[Why is there something rather than
nothing?](/wiki/Why_is_there_something_rather_than_nothing%3F "Why is there
something rather than nothing?")"; "Of what does reality ultimately consist?";
and "Are humans free?"[122]

Metaphysics is sometimes divided into general metaphysics and specific or
special metaphysics. General metaphysics investigates being as such. It
examines the features that all entities have in common. Specific metaphysics
is interested in different kinds of being, the features they have, and how
they differ from one another.[123]

An important area in metaphysics is [ontology](/wiki/Ontology "Ontology").
Some theorists identify it with general metaphysics. Ontology investigates
concepts like [being](/wiki/Being "Being"),
[becoming](/wiki/Becoming_\(philosophy\) "Becoming \(philosophy\)"), and
reality. It studies the [categories of being](/wiki/Categories_of_being
"Categories of being") and asks what exists on the most fundamental
level.[124] Another subfield of metaphysics is [philosophical
cosmology](/wiki/Philosophical_cosmology "Philosophical cosmology"). It is
interested in the essence of the world as a whole. It asks questions including
whether the universe has a beginning and an end and whether it was created by
something else.[125]

A key topic in metaphysics concerns the question of whether reality only
consists of physical things like matter and energy. Alternative suggestions
are that mental entities (such as [souls](/wiki/Soul "Soul") and
[experiences](/wiki/Experience "Experience")) and [abstract
entities](/wiki/Abstract_and_concrete "Abstract and concrete") (such as
numbers) exist apart from physical things. Another topic in metaphysics
concerns the problem of [identity](/wiki/Identity_\(philosophy\) "Identity
\(philosophy\)"). One question is how much an entity can change while still
remaining the same entity.[126] According to one view, entities have
[essential](/wiki/Essence "Essence") and [accidental
features](/wiki/Accident_\(philosophy\) "Accident \(philosophy\)"). They can
change their accidental features but they cease to be the same entity if they
lose an essential feature.[127] A central distinction in metaphysics is
between [particulars](/wiki/Particular "Particular") and
[universals](/wiki/Universals "Universals"). Universals, like the color red,
can exist at different locations at the same time. This is not the case for
particulars including individual persons or specific objects.[128] Other
metaphysical questions are whether the past [fully
determines](/wiki/Determinism "Determinism") the present and what implications
this would have for the existence of [free will](/wiki/Free_will "Free
will").[129]

### Other major branches

See also: [List of philosophies](/wiki/List_of_philosophies "List of
philosophies")

There are many other subfields of philosophy besides its core branches. Some
of the most prominent are aesthetics, philosophy of language, philosophy of
mind, philosophy of religion, philosophy of science, and political
philosophy.[130]

[Aesthetics](/wiki/Aesthetics "Aesthetics") in the philosophical sense is the
field that studies the nature and appreciation of [beauty](/wiki/Beauty
"Beauty") and other aesthetic properties, like [the sublime](/wiki/The_sublime
"The sublime").[131] Although it is often treated together with the
[philosophy of art](/wiki/Aesthetics#Aesthetics_and_the_philosophy_of_art
"Aesthetics"), aesthetics is a broader category that encompasses other aspects
of experience, such as natural beauty.[132] In a more general sense,
aesthetics is "critical reflection on art, culture, and [nature](/wiki/Nature
"Nature")".[133] A key question in aesthetics is whether beauty is an
objective feature of entities or a subjective aspect of experience.[134]
Aesthetic philosophers also investigate the nature of aesthetic experiences
and [judgments](/wiki/Aesthetic_judgment "Aesthetic judgment"). Further topics
include the essence of [works of art](/wiki/Works_of_art "Works of art") and
the processes involved in creating them.[135]

The [philosophy of language](/wiki/Philosophy_of_language "Philosophy of
language") studies the nature and function of [language](/wiki/Language
"Language"). It examines the concepts of
[meaning](/wiki/Meaning_\(philosophy\) "Meaning \(philosophy\)"),
[reference](/wiki/Reference "Reference"), and truth. It aims to answer
questions such as how words are related to things and how language affects
human [thought](/wiki/Thought "Thought") and understanding. It is closely
related to the disciplines of logic and linguistics.[136] The philosophy of
language rose to particular prominence in the early 20th century in [analytic
philosophy](/wiki/Analytic_philosophy "Analytic philosophy") due to the works
of [Frege](/wiki/Frege "Frege") and Russell. One of its central topics is to
understand how sentences get their meaning. There are two broad theoretical
camps: those emphasizing the formal [truth conditions](/wiki/Truth_conditions
"Truth conditions") of sentences[d] and those investigating circumstances that
determine when it is suitable to use a sentence, the latter of which is
associated with [speech act theory](/wiki/Speech_act_theory "Speech act
theory").[138]

The [philosophy of mind](/wiki/Philosophy_of_mind "Philosophy of mind")
studies the nature of mental phenomena and how they are related to the
physical world.[139] It aims to understand different types of
[conscious](/wiki/Conscious "Conscious") and
[unconscious](/wiki/Unconscious_mind "Unconscious mind") [mental
states](/wiki/Mental_states "Mental states"), like [beliefs](/wiki/Belief
"Belief"), [desires](/wiki/Desire "Desire"), [intentions](/wiki/Intention
"Intention"), [feelings](/wiki/Feeling "Feeling"), [sensations](/wiki/Sense
"Sense"), and free will.[140] An influential intuition in the philosophy of
mind is that there is a distinction between the inner experience of objects
and their existence in the external world. The [mind-body problem](/wiki/Mind-
body_problem "Mind-body problem") is the problem of explaining how these two
types of thing—mind and matter—are related. The main traditional responses are
[materialism](/wiki/Materialism "Materialism"), which assumes that matter is
more fundamental; [idealism](/wiki/Idealism "Idealism"), which assumes that
mind is more fundamental; and [dualism](/wiki/Mind%E2%80%93body_dualism
"Mind–body dualism"), which assumes that mind and matter are distinct types of
entities. In contemporary philosophy, another common view is
[functionalism](/wiki/Functionalism_\(philosophy_of_mind\) "Functionalism
\(philosophy of mind\)"), which understands mental states in terms of the
functional or causal roles they play.[141] The mind-body problem is closely
related to the [hard problem of
consciousness](/wiki/Hard_problem_of_consciousness "Hard problem of
consciousness"), which asks how the physical brain can produce [qualitatively
subjective experiences.](/wiki/Qualia "Qualia")[142]

The [philosophy of religion](/wiki/Philosophy_of_religion "Philosophy of
religion") investigates the basic concepts, assumptions, and arguments
associated with [religion](/wiki/Religion "Religion"). It critically reflects
on what religion is, how to define the [divine](/wiki/Divine "Divine"), and
whether one or more gods exist. It also includes the discussion of
[worldviews](/wiki/Worldview "Worldview") that reject religious
doctrines.[143] Further questions addressed by the philosophy of religion are:
"How are we to interpret religious language, if not literally?";[144] "Is
divine omniscience compatible with free will?";[145] and, "Are the great
variety of world religions in some way compatible in spite of their apparently
contradictory theological claims?"[146] It includes topics from nearly all
branches of philosophy.[147] It differs from [theology](/wiki/Theology
"Theology") since theological debates typically take place within one
religious tradition, whereas debates in the philosophy of religion transcend
any particular set of theological assumptions.[148]

The [philosophy of science](/wiki/Philosophy_of_science "Philosophy of
science") examines the fundamental concepts, assumptions, and problems
associated with science. It reflects on what science is and how to distinguish
it from [pseudoscience](/wiki/Pseudoscience "Pseudoscience"). It investigates
the methods employed by scientists, how their application can result in
knowledge, and on what assumptions they are based. It also studies the purpose
and implications of science.[149] Some of its questions are "What counts as an
adequate explanation?";[150] "Is a scientific law anything more than a
description of a regularity?";[151] and "Can some special sciences be
explained entirely in the terms of a more general science?"[152] It is a vast
field that is commonly divided into the philosophy of the [natural
sciences](/wiki/Natural_sciences "Natural sciences") and the philosophy of the
[social sciences](/wiki/Social_sciences "Social sciences"), with further
subdivisions for each of the individual sciences under these headings. How
these branches are related to one another is also a question in the philosophy
of science. Many of its philosophical issues overlap with the fields of
metaphysics or epistemology.[153]

[Political philosophy](/wiki/Political_philosophy "Political philosophy") is
the philosophical inquiry into the fundamental principles and ideas governing
political systems and societies. It examines the basic concepts, assumptions,
and arguments in the field of [politics](/wiki/Politics "Politics"). It
investigates the nature and purpose of [government](/wiki/Government
"Government") and compares its different forms.[154] It further asks under
what circumstances the use of political power is
[legitimate](/wiki/Political_legitimacy "Political legitimacy"), rather than a
form of simple violence.[155] In this regard, it is concerned with the
distribution of political power, social and material goods, and [legal
rights](/wiki/Legal_rights "Legal rights").[156] Other topics are
[justice](/wiki/Justice "Justice"), [liberty](/wiki/Liberty "Liberty"),
[equality](/wiki/Social_equality "Social equality"),
[sovereignty](/wiki/Sovereignty "Sovereignty"), and
[nationalism](/wiki/Nationalism "Nationalism").[157] Political philosophy
involves a general inquiry into normative matters and differs in this respect
from [political science](/wiki/Political_science "Political science"), which
aims to provide empirical descriptions of actually existing states.[158]
Political philosophy is often treated as a subfield of ethics.[159]
Influential schools of thought in political philosophy are
[liberalism](/wiki/Liberalism "Liberalism"),
[conservativism](/wiki/Conservativism "Conservativism"),
[socialism](/wiki/Socialism "Socialism"), and [anarchism](/wiki/Anarchism
"Anarchism").[160]

## Methods

Main article: [Philosophical methodology](/wiki/Philosophical_methodology
"Philosophical methodology")

Methods of philosophy are ways of conducting philosophical inquiry. They
include techniques for arriving at philosophical knowledge and justifying
philosophical claims as well as principles used for choosing between competing
theories.[161] A great variety of methods have been employed throughout the
history of philosophy. Many of them differ significantly from the methods used
in the [natural sciences](/wiki/Natural_sciences "Natural sciences") in that
they do not use experimental data obtained through measuring equipment.[162]
The choice of one's method usually has important implications both for how
philosophical theories are constructed and for the arguments cited for or
against them.[163] This choice is often guided by epistemological
considerations about what constitutes philosophical [evidence](/wiki/Evidence
"Evidence").[164]

Methodological disagreements can cause conflicts among philosophical theories
or about the answers to philosophical questions. The discovery of new methods
has often had important consequences both for how philosophers conduct their
research and for what claims they defend.[165] Some philosophers engage in
most of their theorizing using one particular method while others employ a
wider range of methods based on which one fits the specific problem
investigated best.[166]

Conceptual analysis is a common method in analytic philosophy. It aims to
clarify the meaning of concepts by analyzing them into their component
parts.[167] Another method often employed in analytic philosophy is based on
[common sense](/wiki/Common_sense "Common sense"). It starts with commonly
accepted beliefs and tries to draw unexpected conclusions from them, which it
often employs in a negative sense to criticize philosophical theories that are
too far removed from how the average person sees the issue.[168] It is similar
to how [ordinary language philosophy](/wiki/Ordinary_language_philosophy
"Ordinary language philosophy") approaches philosophical questions by
investigating how ordinary language is used.[169]

[![Diagram depicting a trolley that is headed towards a group of people. There
is an alternate track with only one person and a switch to change
tracks.](//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Trolley_Problem.svg/330px-
Trolley_Problem.svg.png)](/wiki/File:Trolley_Problem.svg)The [trolley
problem](/wiki/Trolley_problem "Trolley problem") is a thought experiment that
investigates the moral difference between doing and allowing harm. This issue
is explored in an imaginary situation in which a person can sacrifice a single
person by redirecting a trolley to save a group of people.[170]

Various methods in philosophy give particular importance to
[intuitions](/wiki/Intuition "Intuition"), that is, non-inferential
impressions about the correctness of specific claims or general
principles.[171] For example, they play an important role in [thought
experiments](/wiki/Thought_experiment "Thought experiment"), which employ
[counterfactual thinking](/wiki/Counterfactual_thinking "Counterfactual
thinking") to evaluate the possible consequences of an imagined situation.
These anticipated consequences can then be used to confirm or refute
philosophical theories.[172] The method of [reflective
equilibrium](/wiki/Reflective_equilibrium "Reflective equilibrium") also
employs intuitions. It seeks to form a [coherent](/wiki/Coherentism
"Coherentism") position on a certain issue by examining all the relevant
beliefs and intuitions, some of which often have to be deemphasized or
reformulated to arrive at a coherent perspective.[173]

Pragmatists stress the significance of concrete practical consequences for
assessing whether a philosophical theory is true.[174] According to the
[pragmatic maxim](/wiki/Pragmatic_maxim "Pragmatic maxim") as formulated by
[Charles Sanders Peirce](/wiki/Charles_Sanders_Peirce "Charles Sanders
Peirce"), the idea a person has of an object is nothing more than the totality
of practical consequences they associate with this object. Pragmatists have
also used this method to expose disagreements as merely verbal, that is, to
show they make no genuine difference on the level of consequences.[175]

Phenomenologists seek knowledge of the realm of appearance and the structure
of human experience. They insist upon the first-personal character of all
experience and proceed by suspending theoretical judgments about the external
world. This technique of phenomenological reduction is known as "bracketing"
or [epoché](/wiki/Bracketing_\(phenomenology\) "Bracketing
\(phenomenology\)"). The goal is to give an unbiased description of the
appearance of things.[176]

[Methodological naturalism](/wiki/Methodological_naturalism "Methodological
naturalism") places great emphasis on the empirical approach and the resulting
theories found in the natural sciences. In this way, it contrasts with
methodologies that give more weight to pure reasoning and introspection.[177]

## Relation to other fields

[![Photo of Judith
Butler](//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/JudithButler2013_%28cropped%29.jpg/250px-
JudithButler2013_%28cropped%29.jpg)](/wiki/File:JudithButler2013_\(cropped\).jpg)[Judith
Butler](/wiki/Judith_Butler "Judith Butler") is one of the philosophers
responsible for the cultural influence of philosophy on the feminist movement.

Philosophy is closely related to many other fields. It is sometimes understood
as a meta-discipline that clarifies their nature and limits. It does this by
critically examining their basic concepts, background assumptions, and
methods. In this regard, it plays a key role in providing an
[interdisciplinary](/wiki/Interdisciplinary "Interdisciplinary") perspective.
It bridges the gap between different disciplines by analyzing which concepts
and problems they have in common. It shows how they overlap while also
delimiting their scope.[178] Historically, most of the individual sciences
originated from philosophy.[179]

The influence of philosophy is felt in several fields that require difficult
practical decisions. In [medicine](/wiki/Medicine "Medicine"), philosophical
considerations related to [bioethics](/wiki/Bioethics "Bioethics") affect
issues like whether an [embryo](/wiki/Embryo "Embryo") is already a
[person](/wiki/Personhood "Personhood") and under what conditions
[abortion](/wiki/Abortion "Abortion") is morally permissible. A closely
related philosophical problem is how humans should treat other animals, for
instance, whether it is acceptable to use non-human animals as food or for
[research experiments](/wiki/Animal_experiments "Animal experiments").[180] In
relation to [business](/wiki/Business "Business") and professional life,
philosophy has contributed by providing ethical frameworks. They contain
guidelines on which business practices are morally acceptable and cover the
issue of [corporate social
responsibility](/wiki/Corporate_social_responsibility "Corporate social
responsibility").[181]

Philosophical inquiry is relevant to many fields that are concerned with what
to believe and how to arrive at evidence for one's beliefs.[182] This is a key
issue for the sciences, which have as one of their prime objectives the
creation of scientific knowledge. Scientific knowledge is based on [empirical
evidence](/wiki/Empirical_evidence "Empirical evidence") but it is often not
clear whether empirical observations are neutral or already [include
theoretical assumptions](/wiki/Theory-ladenness "Theory-ladenness"). A closely
connected problem is whether the available [evidence is
sufficient](/wiki/Underdetermination "Underdetermination") to decide between
competing theories.[183] Epistemological problems in relation to the
[law](/wiki/Law "Law") include what counts as evidence and how much evidence
is required to find a person [guilty](/wiki/Guilt_\(law\) "Guilt \(law\)") of
a crime. A related issue in [journalism](/wiki/Journalism "Journalism") is how
to ensure truth and [objectivity](/wiki/Journalistic_objectivity "Journalistic
objectivity") when reporting on events.[178]

In the fields of [theology](/wiki/Theology "Theology") and religion, there are
many doctrines associated with the existence and nature of God as well as
rules governing correct behavior. A key issue is whether a rational person
should believe these doctrines, for example, whether
[revelation](/wiki/Revelation "Revelation") in the form of holy books and
[religious experiences](/wiki/Religious_experience "Religious experience") of
the divine are sufficient evidence for these beliefs.[184]

Philosophy in the form of logic has been influential in the fields of
mathematics and [computer science](/wiki/Computer_science "Computer
science").[185] Further fields influenced by philosophy include
[psychology](/wiki/Psychology "Psychology"), [sociology](/wiki/Sociology
"Sociology"), linguistics, [education](/wiki/Education "Education"), and [the
arts](/wiki/The_arts "The arts").[186] The close relation between philosophy
and other fields in the contemporary period is reflected in the fact that many
philosophy graduates go on to work in related fields rather than in philosophy
itself.[187]

In the field of politics, philosophy addresses issues such as how to assess
whether a government policy is just.[188] Philosophical ideas have prepared
and shaped various political developments. For example, ideals formulated in
[Enlightenment philosophy](/wiki/Enlightenment_philosophy "Enlightenment
philosophy") laid the foundation for [constitutional
democracy](/wiki/Constitutional_democracy "Constitutional democracy") and
played a role in the [American Revolution](/wiki/American_Revolution "American
Revolution") and the [French Revolution](/wiki/French_Revolution "French
Revolution").[189] Marxist philosophy and its exposition of communism was one
of the factors in the [Russian Revolution](/wiki/Russian_Revolution "Russian
Revolution") and the [Chinese Communist
Revolution](/wiki/Chinese_Communist_Revolution "Chinese Communist
Revolution").[190] In India, [Mahatma Gandhi](/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi "Mahatma
Gandhi")'s [philosophy of non-violence](/wiki/Satyagraha#Ahimsa_and_satyagraha
"Satyagraha") shaped the [Indian independence
movement](/wiki/Indian_independence_movement "Indian independence
movement").[191]

An example of the cultural and critical role of philosophy is found in its
influence on the [feminist](/wiki/Feminist "Feminist") movement through
philosophers such as [Mary Wollstonecraft](/wiki/Mary_Wollstonecraft "Mary
Wollstonecraft"), [Simone de Beauvoir](/wiki/Simone_de_Beauvoir "Simone de
Beauvoir"), and [Judith Butler](/wiki/Judith_Butler "Judith Butler"). It has
shaped the understanding of key concepts in feminism, for instance, the
meaning of [gender](/wiki/Gender "Gender"), how it differs from [biological
sex](/wiki/Biological_sex "Biological sex"), and what role it plays in the
formation of [personal identity](/wiki/Personal_identity "Personal identity").
Philosophers have also investigated the concepts of justice and
[equality](/wiki/Social_equality "Social equality") and their implications
with respect to the [prejudicial treatment of women](/wiki/Sexism "Sexism") in
[male-dominated societies](/wiki/Patriarchy "Patriarchy").[192]

The idea that philosophy is useful for many aspects of life and society is
sometimes rejected. According to one such view, philosophy is mainly
undertaken for its own sake and does not make significant contributions to
existing practices or external goals.[193]
